Compressible Navier-Stokes-Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert system: derivations and well-posedness Thanks: August 9, 2026
Boling Guo, Ning Jiang, Hui Liu, Yi-Long Luo, Teng-Fei Zhang

Abstract
. In this paper, we first derive the compressible Navier-Stokes/Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (NS-LLG) model for magnetoelastic materials via the energetic variational approach (EnVarA). It is important to emphasize that the manner in which the evolution of magnetoelastic materials is influenced by the fluid motion–specifically through the deformation gradient–determines the kinematics of the magnetization and consequently leads to distinct governing equations. Subsequently, we establish the local-in-time existence of solutions to the compressible NS-LLG system under finite initial energy. Finally, near the constant equilibrium for magnetoelasticity in the absence of an external magnetic field, we reformulate the evolutionary model, which allows an additional dissipative term to be identified from the elastic stress. Based on this reformulation, we justify the global well-posedness of the evolutionary magnetoelasticity system with zero external magnetic field, provided the initial data are sufficiently small. In particular, when the magnetic field M M vanishes, this model reduces to the viscoelastic model. Our results significantly relax the previous initial data requirements, only assume the most basic structural condition ρ 0 ​ det ⁡ F 0 = 1 \rho_{0}\operatorname{det}F_{0}=1 . Keywords. Compressible Navier-Stokes/Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert model; Magnetoelasticity; Energetic Variational Approach; Global classical solutions AMS subject classifications. 35B45, 35B65, 35Q35, 76D03, 76D09, 76D10



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
A magnetoelastic model: the compressible Navier-Stokes-Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert system

The phenomenon of magnetoelasticity, describing the interaction between elastic and magnetic effects, was identified as early as the 1960s (see
[
38
]
). In a typical manifestation, applying a magnetizing field to a ferromagnetic rod alters not only its magnetization but also its length. Conversely, subjecting the rod to tension changes both its length and its magnetization. Early constitutive modeling of such materials was developed by Brown
[
38
]
and Tiersten
[
39
,
40
]
. From an analytical perspective, much of the existing work focuses on static configurations via energy minimization
[
48
,
50
,
21
]
. For dynamic, rate-independent evolution, the framework of energetic solutions has been employed
[
26
,
37
]
. In micromagnetics, the dynamics is typically governed by the Landau–Lifshitz–Gilbert (LLG) equation
[
18
,
20
,
28
]
, whose analytical theory is well-developed (see, e.g.,
[
16
,
45
,
35
,
36
]
). Coupling the LLG equation with elasticity in the small-strain regime has also been studied
[
44
,
46
]
. Very recently, a thermodynamic model for viscoelastic deformable magnetic materials was proposed in
[
42
]
. That model incorporates the Landau theory for ferro-para-magnetic phase transition, a gradient theory for magnetization with a mechanically dependent coefficient, hysteresis in the magnetization evolution via an objective corotational time derivative in the LLG equation, and the effects of the demagnetizing field.

We begin by deriving a compressible Navier-Stokes/Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert system using the energetic variational approach (EnVarA). The resulting system of equations is as follows:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho v)=0\,,\\
\begin{aligned} \rho(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v)+\;&\nabla\left(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right)\\[5.0pt]
&=\nabla\cdot\left(\Sigma(v)-2A\nabla M\odot\nabla M+\tfrac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{\top}}{\det F}\right)+\mu_{0}(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M\,,\end{aligned}\\
\partial_{t}F+(v\cdot\nabla)F=\nabla vF\,,\\
\partial_{t}M+v\cdot\nabla M=-\gamma M\times(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})-\lambda M\times M\times(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})\,,\\
|M(x,t)|=1\,.\end{cases}

for
(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\Omega
. Here
\Sigma(v)=\mu(\nabla v+\nabla^{T}v)+\xi(\nabla\cdot v)I
denotes the viscosity dissipation tensor. The scalar function
W(F)
represents the elastic energy, with
W^{\prime}(F)
being the Piola-Kirchhoff tensor; consequently,
\frac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{\top}}{\det F}
is the corresponding Cauchy-Green stress tensor. The exterior magnetic field
H_{ext}(t,x)
is a prescribed vector-valued function. The material motion is described by the map
x=x(X,t)
, defined as the solution to

(1.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}t}=v(x(X,t),t),\quad x(X,0)=X.

Associated to the flow, we introduce the deformation gradient tensor
F
(the Jacobian of the flow map):

(1.3)

\displaystyle F(X,t)=\frac{\partial x(X,t)}{\partial X},\quad\text{or, in components},\ F_{ij}=\frac{\partial x_{i}}{\partial X_{j}}.

For compressible flows, the relation
\rho\det F=1
holds, which reduces to
\det F=1
in incompressible case. We consider a polytropic (or
\gamma
-law) pressure for the compressible fluid, i.e.,
P(\rho)=a\rho^{\gamma}
with
\gamma>1
, which corresponds to the internal energy density

w(\rho)=\frac{a}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}.

The physical parameters in (
1.1
) satisfy

\displaystyle a>0,\ \gamma>1,\ A>0,\ \lambda>0,\ \mu>0,\ \mu+\xi>0\,.

The compressible system (
1.8
) is derived by applying the Energetic Variational Approach (EnVarA) developed by C. Liu and collaborators; see, e.g.,
[
5
,
52
]
for a comprehensive exposition. Following this framework, we define the total energy as the sum of the kinetic energy
\mathcal{K}
and the Helmholtz free energy
\mathcal{F}
:

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{F},

where

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{t}}\tfrac{1}{2}\rho|v|^{2}\mathrm{d}x,

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{t}}\left[w(\rho)+A|\nabla M|^{2}+\frac{W(F)}{\det F}-\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right]\mathrm{d}x\,.

Here,
\Omega^{t}
denotes the current configuration. The dissipation in the system is characterized by the functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(v)+\mathcal{D}(M)

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left[\mu|\nabla v|^{2}+(\mu+\xi)|\nabla\cdot v|^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}x\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(M)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\lambda|M\times(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\,.

A central kinematic assumption, following Bene
\check{\mathrm{s}}
ová-Forster-Liu-Schlömerkemper
[
1
]
, is that the magnetization satisfies the Lagrangian condition
M(x(X,t),\ t)=M(X,t)
. Physically, this means the magnetization vector is tied to material points and is transported with the flow without being stretched or rotated independently of the deformation (see
[
1
, Remark 2.1]
). This viewpoint differs from that in, e.g., Forster’s thesis
[
3
]
. Under this assumption, the application of the EnVarA yields the system (
1.1
). The detailed derivation is provided in Section
2
.

When the elastic energy density is given by the Hookean linear elasticity, i.e.,
W(F)=\frac{1}{2}|F|^{2}
, the system (
1.1
) reduces to

(1.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho v)=0\,,\\
\begin{aligned} &\rho(\partial_{t}v+\;v\cdot\nabla v)+\nabla\left(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right)\\[5.0pt]
&=\mu\Delta v+(\mu+\xi)\nabla(\nabla\cdot v)+\nabla\cdot(\rho FF^{\top})-2A\nabla\cdot(\nabla M\odot\nabla M)+\mu_{0}(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M\,,\end{aligned}\\
\partial_{t}F+(v\cdot\nabla)F=\nabla vF\,,\\
\partial_{t}M+v\cdot\nabla M=2A\lambda\Delta M+\lambda\mu_{0}H_{ext}+\Gamma(M)M-\gamma M\times(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})\,,\\
|M(x,t)|=1\,,\end{cases}

where the Lagrangian multiplier is
\Gamma(M)=2\lambda A|\nabla M|^{2}-\lambda\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}
. The reformulation of the magnetization evolution equation follows the standard procedure; see, for instance,
[
9
]
.

The objective of this paper is to study the system (
1.8
) on the spatial domain
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{3}
, subject to the initial conditions

(1.9)

\displaystyle(\rho,v,F,M)(0,x)=(\rho_{0},v_{0},F_{0},M_{0})(x)

with
|M_{0}|=1
.

1.2.
Main results

Before stating the main results, we introduce the notation used throughout the paper. We denote by
L^{p}(\Omega)
the standard Lebesgue space, abbreviated as
L^{p}
for
p\in[1,\infty]
. Its norm is given by

\|f\|_{L^{p}}=\left(\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}(1\leq p<\infty)\,,\quad\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|\,.

For
p=2
, the inner product on
L^{2}
is written as
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. The unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is denoted by
\mathbb{S}^{2}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3};|v|=1\}
. The spatial gradient and divergence are denoted by
\nabla
and
\nabla\cdot
, respectively. We write
\partial_{i}=\partial_{x_{i}}
for
i=1,2,3
, and the Laplacian is
\Delta=\partial_{i}\partial_{i}
(summation convention used). For a matrix-valued function
G=(G^{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}
, its divergence is defined componentwise by
(\nabla\cdot G)_{i}=\partial_{j}G^{ji}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
.

Given a multi-index
m=(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, we set
partial^{m}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{m_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}^{m_{2}}\partial_{x_{3}}^{m_{3}}
and
|m|=m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}
. For two multi-indices
m,\widetilde{m}
, the notation
m\leq\widetilde{m}
means
m_{i}\leq\widetilde{m}_{i}
for all
i
, the strict inequality
m<\widetilde{m}
means
m\leq\widetilde{m}
and
|m|<|\widetilde{m}|
.

The Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
(abbreviated
W^{s,p}
) is equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{W^{s,p}}=\left(\sum_{|m|\leq s}\int_{\Omega}|\partial^{m}f(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

When
p=2
, we write
H^{s}(\Omega)=W^{s,2}(\Omega)
. For a given positive weight function
\rho:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the weighted spaces
L^{p}_{\rho}
,
W^{s,p}_{\rho}
and
H^{s}_{\rho}
are defined analogously by replacing the Lebesgue measure
\mathrm{d}x
with the weighted measure
\rho(x)\mathrm{d}x
in the corresponding norms.

For any integer
s\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the following local energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and local dissipation-rate functional
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
:

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)

\displaystyle:=\|\rho\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|F\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|v\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\rho}}+\|\nabla M\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mu\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+(\mu+\xi)\|\nabla\cdot v\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+2\lambda A\|\Delta M\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,.

Correspondingly, the initial local energy is

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}^{in}

\displaystyle:=\|\rho_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|F_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|v_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\rho_{0}}}+\|\nabla M_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,.

With these preliminaries, we can now state our first main theorem.

Theorem 1.1

(Local well-posedness)
.

Let
s\geq 3
be an integer. Suppose the initial data
(\rho_{0},v_{0},F_{0},M_{0})
in (
1.9
) satisfy

\mathcal{E}_{s}^{in}<\infty,\quad\inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}}\rho_{0}(x)>0\,.

Assume further that the external magnetic field satisfies
H_{ext}\in L^{\infty}(0,T_{0};H^{s})
for some
T_{0}>0
. Then there exists a time
T\in(0,T_{0}]
such that the Cauchy problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) admits a unique solution
(\rho,v,F,M)
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
with the following properties:

\displaystyle|M(t,x)|=1,\quad\inf_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}}\rho(t,x)>0,

\displaystyle\rho,F,v,\nabla M\in L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3})),\nabla v,\Delta M\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))\,.

Moreover, the solution satisfies the energy estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)dt\leq C

where the constant
C>0
depends on
s
,
T
and
\mathcal{E}_{s}^{in}
.

We now consider the global well-posedness of (
1.8
) near the constant equilibrium
(1,0,I,M_{e})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
in the absence of an external magnetic field, i.e.,
H_{ext}=0
, on the spatial domain
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
. Introducing the fluctuation variables

\displaystyle\rho=1+\theta\,,\quad M=d+M_{e}\,,\quad v=u\,,

and using the constraint
|M|=|M_{e}|=1
, which implies
|d|^{2}+2M_{e}\cdot d=0
, we obtain from (
1.8
) the following system for
(\theta,u,F,d)
:

(1.12)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\theta+u\cdot\nabla\theta+(1+\theta)\nabla\cdot u=0\,,\\
\begin{aligned} (1&+\theta)(\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u)+\nabla\left(P(1+\theta)-A|\nabla d|^{2}\right)\\[5.0pt]
&=\mu\Delta u+(\mu+\xi)\nabla(\nabla\cdot u)+\nabla\cdot[(1+\theta)FF^{\top}]-2A\nabla\cdot(\nabla d\odot\nabla d)\,,\end{aligned}\\
\partial_{t}F+(u\cdot\nabla)F=\nabla uF\,,\\
\partial_{t}d+u\cdot\nabla d=2A\lambda\Delta d+2A\lambda|\nabla d|^{2}(d+M_{e})-2A\gamma(d+M_{e})\times\Delta d\,,\\
|d|^{2}+2M_{e}\cdot d=0\,.\end{cases}

Define the global energy functional

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{s}(t)=\|\theta\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|u\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+|\bar{d}|^{2}+\|d-\bar{d}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla d\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|F^{-1}-I\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,,

and the corresponding global dissipation-rate functional

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{s}(t)=\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla\theta\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|F^{-1}-I|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla d\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\Delta d\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla\cdot u\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,.

The associated initial energy is

(1.15)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{s}^{in}=\|\rho_{0}-1\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|v_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+|\overline{M_{0}-M_{e}}|^{2}+\|M_{0}-\overline{M_{0}}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla M_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|F^{-1}_{0}-I\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,,

where the overline denotes the spatial average:

(1.16)

\displaystyle\bar{f}(t)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}^{3}|}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}f(t,x)\mathrm{d}x.

With the above notations, we state the following global well-posedness result.

Theorem 1.2

(Global well-posedness)
.

Let
s\geq 3
be an integer and assume the external magnetic field is absent,
H_{ext}=0
. Let the initial data satisfy the compressibility conditions

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\det F_{0}=1\,,\inf_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}}\rho_{0}(x)>0.

Then there exists a constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
, sufficiently small, such that if

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{s}^{in}\leq\epsilon_{0}\,,

the perturbed system (
1.12
) admits a unique global-in-time solution
(\theta,u,F,d)
satisfying

\displaystyle\theta,u,d-\bar{d},\nabla d,F^{-1}-I\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{s})\,,\bar{d}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\,,\nabla u,\Delta d\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{s})\,.

Furthermore, there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that the following energy estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathbf{E}_{s}(t)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{D}_{s}(t)dt\leq c_{0}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{in}\,.

1.3.
Historical remarks

The system (
1.1
) represents a nonlinear coupling between the hydrodynamics of viscoelasticity and the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation. While the analytical theory for each subsystem has been extensively developed over the past two decades, the well-posedness analysis of the coupled system (
1.1
) remains largely open. In
[
1
]
, global-in-time weak solutions were constructed via a Galerkin method combined with a fixed-point argument, adapting ideas from the work on liquid crystal flows by Lin and Zhang
[
30
]
and on the Landau-Lifshitz equation by Carbou and Fabrie
[
45
]
. Related progress in this direction can be found in
[
24
,
41
]
. We note that the notion of “weak solution” in
[
1
]
and
[
24
]
is non-standard: the spatial regularity required for the magnetization
M
is not merely
H^{1}
, but higher (
H^{3}
in
[
1
]
and
H^{2}
in
[
24
]
). Moreover, both works consider a regularized version of the transport equation for the deformation gradient, involving a term
\kappa\Delta F
, rather than the physical case
\kappa=0
corresponding to incompressible version of system (
1.1
). Additionally, these results require the initial deviation of
F
from the identity matrix
I
to be small. These restrictive assumptions stem from fundamental analytical difficulties: the lack of regularity for the transport equation and the geometric constraint
|M(t,x)|=1
, which is notoriously difficult to preserve under weak approximations. In fact, handling this constraint remains highly nontrivial even in the higher-regularity framework adopted in the present paper. We also mention the work
[
41
]
, where a local well-posedness and a blow-up criterion for classical solutions are established for a modified version of (
1.1
)—specifically, with the constraint
|M|=1
replaced by a Ginzburg-Landau penalization. In contrast, Jiang, Liu, and Luo
[
9
]
studied the incompressible counterpart of the physical system (
1.1
) while preserving the geometric constraint
|M(t,x)|=1
. Working in the setting of classical solutions rather than the weak-strong framework of
[
1
,
24
]
, they established the first global existence theory for classical solutions to the incompressible version of the full evolutionary magnetoelastic system (
1.1
).

We note that by removing the magnetization vector field
M(t,x)
, the system (
1.1
) reduces to a compressible viscoelastic fluid system of Oldroyd type, which describes a class of non-Newtonian fluids exhibiting phenomena such as elastic memory effects; see, e.g.,
[
17
,
19
,
22
,
29
,
33
]
. In such fluids, the elastic response is captured by an energy functional that depends on the deformation gradient tensor
F
.

The incompressible counterpart of this viscoelastic model has been extensively studied. The local well-posedness in the Sobolev space
H^{s}
was established by Lin et al.
[
29
]
, Chen and Zhang
[
47
]
, Lei et al.
[
25
]
, and Lin and Zhang
[
31
]
; global well-posedness for small initial data was also proved. While the local theory follows from standard energy estimates, the global result relies on a more delicate analysis that reveals a damping mechanism for the deformation gradient fluctuation
F-I
. A key observation in this analysis is that certain linearly appearing terms are actually of higher order. For example, Lei et al.
[
25
]
identified the identity

\nabla_{k}H^{ij}-\nabla_{j}H^{ik}=H^{lj}\nabla_{l}H^{ik}-H^{lk}\nabla_{l}H^{ij},

where
H=F-I
. This relation implies, in particular, that
\nabla\times H
is a higher-order term.

For the incompressible system augmented with a linear damping term in the evolution equation for the Cauchy-Green strain tensor
FF^{\top}
, Chemin and Masmoudi
[
43
]
established the existence of both local solutions and global small solutions in critical Besov spaces; see also
[
34
]
for related well-posedness results in critical spaces. In the compressible setting, Lei and Zhou
[
23
]
proved the local existence of smooth solutions for the two-dimensional system with initial data that are small perturbations of a general incompressible state. Furthermore, they showed the convergence of these solutions to the global solution of the incompressible equations via a compactness argument. For a compressible viscoelastic system without pressure, Lei et al.
[
27
]
established the global existence of smooth solutions near equilibrium.

The global existence of weak solutions, however, remains an open problem even for the incompressible case. When the contribution of the strain rate (the symmetric part of
\nabla v
) is neglected in the constitutive equation, Lions and Masmoudi
[
32
]
proved the global existence of weak solutions for general initial data. For the full compressible system, Qian and Zhang
[
15
]
first established its well-posedness in the largest possible functional framework–namely, in critical spaces (which, in two dimensions, are very close to the natural spaces for weak solutions). Second, they explicitly characterized the smoothing and damping effects generated by the linearized system with convection terms, a key ingredient in proving global existence for small data. We remark that their analysis relies on the following initial structural conditions:

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\left(\frac{F_{0}^{\top}}{\operatorname{det}F_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{0}F_{0}^{\top}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\operatorname{det}F_{0}

\displaystyle=1,\qquad F_{0}^{lk}\nabla_{l}F_{0}^{ij}-F_{0}^{lj}\nabla_{l}F_{0}^{ik}=0.

It is worth noting that, in order to reveal weak dissipative mechanisms for
F-I
, the works
[
47
,
25
,
29
]
introduced and exploited certain special quantities. Finally, we recall the seminal work of Danchin
[
49
]
, who proved the global well-posedness for the compressible Navier–Stokes equations near equilibrium in critical spaces. Compared with that case, the analysis for viscoelastic fluids must also handle the intricate coupling among the velocity, density, and deformation tensor, while utilizing intrinsic structural properties of the viscoelastic system.

1.4.
Novelties and sketch of proofs

1.4.1.
Novelties

We now describe the novelties of the present paper.

First, we derive the compressible Navier–Stokes/Landau–Lifshitz–Gilbert system (
1.1
) via the energetic variational approach. The derivation begins with the definitions of the kinetic energy
\mathcal{K}
and the Helmholtz free energy
\mathcal{F}
, as introduced in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively. From these, we define the action functional

\mathcal{A}({x})=\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{F}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
{x}={x}(X,t)
denotes the flow map given in (
1.2
). The evolution equation for the velocity field
{v}
is obtained from the principle of force balance, which equates the variational derivative of the action functional with the dissipation due to viscosity. In the
L^{2}
sense, this balance reads

\big(\delta_{x}\mathcal{A}\big)_{L^{2}_{t,x}}=\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{\dot{x}}\mathcal{D}\Big)_{L^{2}_{x}},

hence, (
2.10
) below. Here,
\delta_{x}
denotes the variational derivative with respect to the flow map
x
,
\delta_{\dot{x}}
denotes the derivative with respect to the “virtual velocity”
\dot{x}
, and
\mathcal{D}
is the dissipation functional given in (
1.1
). The resulting equation corresponds to the momentum balance in (
1.1
); see the detailed computation leading to (
1.1
).

The equation for the magnetization
{M}
is derived from a separate force balance associated with its variation. Let
\mathring{M}
denote the total kinematic transport of the magnetization, defined as in (
2.16
) below. The force balance in the
L^{2}
sense is then stated in Remark
2.1
below, namely,

\big(\delta_{{M}}\mathcal{A}\big)_{L^{2}_{t,x}}=\Big(\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{\mathring{M}}\mathcal{D}\Big)_{L^{2}_{x}},

where
\delta_{{M}}
is the variational derivative with respect to
{M}
. This yields the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert type equation for
{M}
in (
1.1
). We remark that
\rho
-equation follows from the mass conservation law, and the
F
-equation is a consequence of the flow map (
1.2
).

Second, for the compressible viscoelastic counterpart, the only initial structural condition required to prove the global-in-time result near the constant equilibrium is
\rho_{0}\det F_{0}=1
. Indeed, if one removes the magnetization
M
, the model (
1.1
) reduces to the exact compressible viscoelastic fluid system of Oldroyd type. In the previous work
[
15
]
, the following initial structural conditions are imposed:

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\left(\frac{F_{0}^{\top}}{\det F_{0}}\right)=0,\quad\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{0}F_{0}^{\top}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\det F_{0}=1,F_{0}^{lk}\nabla_{l}F_{0}^{ij}-F_{0}^{lj}\nabla_{l}F_{0}^{ik}=0.

These conditions simplify the compressible viscoelastic fluid system to a hyperbolic–parabolic system, for which global well-posedness with small initial data can be established in critical Besov spaces. In the present paper, we only require the initial structural condition
\rho_{0}\det F_{0}=1
, which implies
\rho\det F=1
for all
t\geq 0
. Since
\rho>0
, we have
\det F>0
. We focus on the fluctuation variable
U=F^{-1}-I
, which satisfies the curl-free property
\partial_{i}U^{jk}=\partial_{k}U^{ji}
and is subject to the evolution (
4.1
) below. Then the div–curl theorem yields
U^{ij}=\partial_{i}\psi^{j}
. Consequently, the evolution of
F
can be transformed into the third equation in (
4.6
), namely,

\partial_{t}\psi+u+u\cdot\nabla\psi=0\,.

Moreover, the divergence of the Cauchy–Green stress tensor for Hookean linear elasticity,

\nabla\cdot(\rho FF^{\top})=-\Delta\psi+\ \text{some higher order terms}\,,

(see (
4.5
) below) provides the required dissipative effect
-\Delta\psi
for the viscoelastic part.

Third, in proving global existence, we need to design the instant energy functional
\mathbb{E}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
(see (
4.10
)) and the instant dissipation rate
\mathbb{D}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
(see (
4.11
)) for the compressible model (
1.1
), which differs from the incompressible NS-LLG model studied in
[
9
]
. To derive the dissipation effect of
\psi
in the energy estimate, we rewrite the
\psi
-equation in the form

\partial_{t}\Delta\psi+\frac{1}{\mu}\Delta\psi+\frac{1}{\mu}\Delta w=-\Delta(u\cdot\nabla\psi)

with
w=\mu(u-\bar{u})-(\psi-\bar{\psi})
. Then the quantity
w
requires further controlled. Using the
u
-equation in (
4.6
),
w
satisfies

-\Delta w=(\mu+\xi)\nabla(\nabla\cdot u)-\nabla P(1+\theta)+F_{com},

where
F_{com}
is readily controlled. In the incompressible model,
w
satisfies the Stokes system

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta w+\nabla q&=F_{{incom}},\\
\nabla\cdot w&=-\nabla\cdot\psi,\end{aligned}\right.

where the source term
F_{{incom}}
is controllable, and
\nabla\cdot\psi=\operatorname{tr}U
is in fact nonlinear due to the incompressibility. Because of the linear effect contained in
(\mu+\xi)\nabla(\nabla\cdot u)-\nabla P(1+\theta)
in the compressible model, the previous ideas fail in the present work. We will instead employ the relation

-\Delta\psi\sim\partial_{t}u+\nabla\theta-\mu\Delta u+(\mu+\xi)\nabla(\nabla\cdot u)+\cdots

derived from the
u
-equation in (
4.6
) to control
w
. More precisely, we apply the relation (
5.5
) to handle the quantity
C_{2}
in (
5.70
), which motivates the design of the instant energy functional and instant dissipation rate in (
4.10
)-(
4.11
). Together with Lemma
4.1
, this allows us to close the global energy estimate (
4.26
) in Proposition
4.1
.

Fourth, owing to compressibility, we estimate separately the average part
\bar{d}
and the zero-mean part
d-\bar{d}
of the fluctuation
d=M-M_{e}
of the magnetization. Indeed,
d
satisfies

\partial_{t}d+u\cdot\nabla d=2A\lambda\Delta d+\text{nonlinear terms}\,.

When establishing the
L^{2}
-estimate, the quantity

\langle u\cdot{\nabla}{d},d\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\langle{\nabla}\cdot u,|d|^{2}\rangle

has to admit the upper bound
\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\mathbf{D}_{s}(t)
. For the incompressible model, this quantity vanishes because
\nabla\cdot u=0
. The dissipation
\mathbf{D}_{s}(t)
involves only
\|\nabla d\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\|\Delta d\|_{H^{s}}^{2}
(the dissipative effect for the magnetization). However,
\|d\|_{L^{4}}^{2}
does not admit the upper bound
\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\|\nabla d\|_{L^{2}}
(
\lesssim\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\mathbf{D}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)
), because the average
\bar{d}
does not vanish (the Poincaré inequality fails). Inspired by the micro-macro decomposition method developed for the hydrodynamic limits of kinetic equations (see
[
6
]
for the Boltzmann equation and
[
7
,
12
]
for the Vlasov–Maxwell–Boltzmann equations, for instance), we estimate the average part
\bar{d}
and the zero-mean part
d-\bar{d}
separately, corresponding respectively to the macroscopic and microscopic parts in kinetic theory. The key observation is the equation (
5.2
) for
\bar{d}
, i.e.,

\partial_{t}\bar{d}=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}^{3}|}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}(\nabla\cdot u)({d}-\bar{d})\mathrm{d}x+\frac{2A\lambda}{|\mathbb{T}^{3}|}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}|\nabla d|^{2}(d-\bar{d}+M_{e}+\bar{d})\mathrm{d}x,

where the right-hand side is nonlinear. This is the required property.

1.4.2.
Sketch of proofs

The first result of this paper is the local well-posedness of (
1.1
) with large smooth initial data. We first derive the local-in-time a priori estimate (
3.1
) in Proposition
3.1
by employing the nonlinear energy approach. Due to the nonlinear constraint
|M(t,x)|=1
for the magnetization, we carefully design a nonlinear iterative approximation scheme (
3.64
) for the NS-LLG system (
1.1
). The nonlinear iterative approximation scheme was initially developed in
[
8
]
to study the local well-posedness of the hyperbolic Ericksen-Leslie liquid crystal model. It was then successfully applied in the works
[
9
,
11
]
. Based on the approximation (
3.64
) and the a priori estimate (
3.1
), we establish the local well-posedness of (
1.1
) by a continuity argument.

The second result of the present work is to establish the global-in-time existence of solutions to (
1.1
) in the absence of an external magnetic field and near a constant equilibrium. As stated in the second part of Subsection
1.4.1
, we exploit the additional damping effect of the deformation gradient
F
by employing the curl-free property of
F^{-1}-I
together with the initial structural condition
\rho_{0}\det F_{0}=1
. More precisely, the damping effect originates from the Cauchy-Green stress tensor
\nabla\cdot(\rho FF^{\top})
for Hookean linear elasticity. We then focus on the reformulated system (
4.6
), which features an additional explicit damping structure
\Delta\psi
.

Our next goal is to derive the global energy estimate (
4.26
) in Proposition
4.1
for the instant energy functional
\mathbb{E}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
and the instant dissipation rate
\mathbb{D}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
. As stated in the last part of Subsection
1.4.1
, we estimate separately the average part
\bar{d}
and the zero-average part
d-\bar{d}
of the fluctuated magnetization
d
. These estimates are established in Lemma
5.1
and Lemma
5.2
, respectively. Moreover, we carry out the estimate of
\nabla{d}
in Lemma
5.3
, thereby completing the estimate for
d
.

We then derive the estimate for
\theta
(the fluctuated density) in Lemma
5.4
and the estimate for
u
(the velocity) in Lemma
5.5
. We emphasize that there is a quantity
\sum_{|m|\leq s}\langle P^{\prime}(1+\theta)\nabla\partial^{m}\theta,\partial^{m}u\rangle
, which cancels in the
\theta
and
u
estimates. Moreover, the interaction dissipation rate
-\sum_{|m|\leq s}\langle\nabla\partial^{m}\psi,\nabla\partial^{m}u\rangle
is contained in the
u
-estimate. However, in order to obtain the damping effect for
\theta
from the pressure gradient
\nabla P(1+\theta)
, we establish Lemma
5.6
. At this stage, the damping structure
a\gamma\|\nabla\theta\|^{2}_{H^{s-1}_{\mathfrak{w}(\theta)}}
is obtained. The cost, however, is the generation of the interaction energy
\|u+\nabla\theta\|_{H^{s-1}}^{2}-\|u\|_{H^{s-1}}^{2}-\|\nabla\theta\|_{H^{s-1}}^{2}
and the interaction dissipation rates

-c_{1}\|\nabla\psi\|_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\theta\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mathfrak{w}(\theta)}}\quad\text{and}\quad-c_{0}\bigl[\mu\|\nabla u\|_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\theta\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mathfrak{w}(\theta)}}+(\mu+\xi)\|\nabla\cdot u\|_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\theta\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mathfrak{w}(\theta)}}\bigr].

These interaction quantities are undesirable.

It remains to estimate the unknown
\psi
(corresponding to the deformation gradient tensor
F
). The damping effect
\Delta\psi
is contained in the
u
-equation but not in the
\psi
-equation. In order to exploit the damping
\Delta\psi
, we transfer it to the
\psi
-equation by introducing the function
w=\mu(u-\bar{u})-(\psi-\bar{\psi})
. Consequently, the quantity
C_{2}
associated with
w
(see (
5.70
)) must be controlled using the relation (
5.5
) (the reformulation of the
u
-equation). Unfortunately, this process results in the interaction energy

-\frac{2}{\mu}\sum_{|m|\leq s}\langle\partial^{m}(\psi-\bar{\psi}),\partial^{m}u\rangle

and the interaction dissipation rates

\displaystyle-\sum_{|m|\leq s}\left\langle\nabla\partial^{m}\psi,\nabla\partial^{m}u\right\rangle,\quad-\frac{\mu+\xi}{\mu}\sum_{|m|\leq s}\left\langle\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}\psi,\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}u\right\rangle,

\displaystyle-c_{1}a\gamma\left(\mu\|\nabla u\|_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{H^{s}}\right)\|\nabla\theta\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mathfrak{w}(\theta)}};

see Lemma
5.7
.

In order to control the interaction quantities, we construct the instant energy
\mathbb{E}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
and the instant dissipation rate
\mathbb{D}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
, depending on the parameters
\delta,\eta,\epsilon>0
. In Lemma
4.1
, we show that there exist constants
\delta,\eta,\epsilon>0
such that
\mathbb{E}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
and
\mathbb{D}_{s;\delta,\eta,\epsilon}(t)
are equivalent to the energy
\mathbf{E}_{s}(t)
(see (
4.8
)) and the dissipation rate
\mathbf{D}_{s}(t)
(see (
4.9
)), respectively. Here
\mathbf{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathbf{D}_{s}(t)
are both positive. Consequently, the global estimate (
4.26
) in Proposition
4.1
is established.

Finally, based on (
4.26
), we extend the local solution constructed in Theorem
1.1
to the time interval
[0,\infty)
by a continuity argument. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

1.5.
Organization of this paper

In the next section, we derive the model (
1.1
) using EnVarA. Section
3
provides the proof of local well-posedness of the system (
1.8
). In Section
4
, we first transform the fluctuation system (
1.12
) into the form (
4.6
), which explicitly exhibits the damping mechanism. Then we state the global energy estimate (
4.26
) in Proposition
4.1
and prove the global existence of the fluctuation system (
4.6
) with small initial data. In Subsection
5
, we derive the global energy estimates and thereby prove Proposition
4.1
.

2.
Derivation of system (
1.1
) by the energetic variational approach

2.1.
EnVarA for the compressible Navier-Stokes equations with magnetoelastic mechanics

Define the action functional
\mathcal{A}(x)=\int_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{F})\mathrm{d}t
, where the kinetic energy
\mathcal{K}
and the Helmholtz free energy
\mathcal{F}
are given in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively. In Lagrangian coordinates, the action functional can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(x)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{F})\mathrm{d}t

(2.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{0}(X)|\dot{x}|^{2}\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\left[w\left(\frac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F}\right)+A|\nabla_{X}M(X,t)F^{-1}|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{W(F)}{\det F}-\mu_{0}M(X,t)\cdot H_{ext}(x(X,t))\right]\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t\,,

where
\dot{x}=\dot{x}(X,t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}t}(X,t)
. Using the relation
\rho(x(X,t),t)=\frac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F}
and integration by parts, (
2.1
) yields

(2.2)

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int\mathcal{K}\mathrm{d}t=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\rho_{0}(X)\dot{x}\cdot\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\delta x\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\frac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}v\cdot\delta x\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\rho(x,t)\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}v\cdot\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\langle-\rho(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla_{x}v),\,\delta x\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}.

Here
\delta_{x}
denotes the spatial variation, and
\delta x
is a function such that
x+h\delta x
is a variation of
x
as
h\to 0
. Moreover,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}
denotes the standard
L^{2}
-inner product in
L^{2}_{t,x}
with respect to the variables
t
and
x
.

For the spatial variation of the Helmholtz free energy, we decompose it into four terms:
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{0}+\mathcal{F}_{1}+\mathcal{F}_{2}+\mathcal{F}_{3}
. We then have

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{0}=\int_{\Omega^{t}}w(\rho(x,t))\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega^{0}}w\left(\frac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F}\right)\det F\mathrm{d}x.

The variation of the above expression yields

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathrm{d}t=\

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}w\left(\frac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F}\right)\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\left[-w^{\prime}(\tfrac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F})\frac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F}+w(\tfrac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F})\right]\;\mathrm{tr}(\nabla_{X}(\delta x)F^{-1})\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

(2.4)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\left[-w^{\prime}(\tfrac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F})\frac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F}+w(\tfrac{\rho_{0}(X)}{\det F})\right]\nabla_{x}\cdot(\delta x)\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\left[-w^{\prime}(\rho(x,t))\rho(x,t)+w(\rho(x,t))\right]\nabla_{x}\cdot(\delta x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\nabla\left[w^{\prime}(\rho)\rho-w(\rho)\right]\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\left[w^{\prime}(\rho)\rho-w(\rho)\right],\,\delta x\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}=\left\langle\nabla p(\rho),\,\delta x\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\,,

where we have used the relation
p(\rho)=w^{\prime}(\rho)\rho-w(\rho)=a\rho^{\gamma}
.

Next, we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathrm{d}t=\

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}A|\nabla_{X}M(X,t)F^{-1}|^{2}\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}2A\left[\nabla_{X}M(X,t)F^{-1}\right]:\nabla_{X}M(X,t)\delta_{x}F^{-1}\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}2A|\nabla_{X}M(X,t)F^{-1}|\delta_{x}\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}2A\left[\nabla_{X}M(X,t)F^{-1}\right]:\nabla_{X}M(X,t)\left[-F^{-1}\nabla_{X}(\delta x)F^{-1}\right]\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}A|\nabla_{X}M(X,t)F^{-1}|^{2}\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\nabla_{X}(\delta x)F^{-1}\right]\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}2A\nabla M:[\nabla M(\nabla\delta x)]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}A|\nabla M|^{2}(\nabla\cdot\delta x)\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}2A\nabla\cdot(\nabla M\odot\nabla M)\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}A\nabla(|\nabla M|^{2})\cdot\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\cdot(2A\nabla M\odot\nabla M)-\nabla(A|\nabla M|^{2}),\,\delta x\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}.

A similar argument yields

(2.6)

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathrm{d}t=\

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}W(F)\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}W^{\prime}(F)\delta_{x}\frac{\partial x(X,t)}{\partial X}\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}W^{\prime}(F)\left[\nabla_{X}(\delta x)\right]\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}W^{\prime}(F)\left[F^{\top}\nabla(\delta x)\right]\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\frac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{\top}}{\det F}\left[\nabla(\delta x)\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\nabla\cdot\left(\frac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{\top}}{\det F}\right)\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=\left\langle-\nabla\cdot\left(\frac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{\top}}{\det F}\right),\,\delta x\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\,,

and

(2.7)

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}_{3}\mathrm{d}t=\

\displaystyle-\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\mu_{0}M(X,t)\cdot H_{ext}(x(X,t))\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\mu_{0}M(X,t)\cdot\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}(x(X,t))\delta x\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\mu_{0}M(X,t)\cdot H_{ext}(x(X,t))\delta_{x}\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\mu_{0}M(X,t)\cdot\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}(x(X,t))\delta x\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{0}}\mu_{0}M(X,t)\cdot H_{ext}(x(X,t))\;\mathrm{tr}\left[\nabla_{X}(\delta x)F^{-1}\right]\det F\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\mu_{0}M(x,t)\cdot\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}(x,t)\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\mu_{0}M(x,t)\cdot H_{ext}(x,t)\;[\nabla\cdot(\delta x)]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}(\mu_{0}\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}M)\cdot\delta x\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{t}}\nabla\left(\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right)\cdot(\delta x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left\langle-(\mu_{0}\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}M)+\nabla\left(\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right),\,\delta x\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\,.

Combining (
2.1
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
), and (
2.7
), we obtain

(2.8)

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla\cdot\left(2A\nabla M\odot\nabla M-\tfrac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{\top}}{\det F}\right)-\mu_{0}\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}M,\,\delta x\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\langle\nabla\left(p(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right),\,\delta x\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\,.

On the other hand, for the variation of the dissipation, recalling the viscous dissipation tensor
\Sigma(v)=\mu(\nabla v+\nabla^{\top}v)+\xi(\nabla\cdot v)I
, we obtain

(2.9)

\displaystyle\delta_{v}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{t}}\left\{\mu\nabla v\nabla(\delta v)+(\mu+\xi)(\nabla\cdot v)\;[\nabla\cdot(\delta v)]\right\}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\left\langle-\nabla\cdot\left[\mu\nabla v+(\mu+\xi)(\nabla\cdot v)\mathcal{I}\right],\,\delta v\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}=\left\langle-\nabla\cdot\Sigma(v),\,\delta v\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}\,,

where
\mathcal{D}
is given in (
1.1
). It then follows from the force balance

(2.10)

\displaystyle\left(\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{A}\mathrm{d}t\right)_{L^{2}_{t,x}}=\left(\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{K}\mathrm{d}t\right)_{L^{2}_{t,x}}-\left(\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}\mathrm{d}t\right)_{L^{2}_{t,x}}=\left(\delta_{\dot{x}}(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{D})\right)_{L^{2}_{x}}

that

(2.11)

\rho(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v)+\nabla\left(p(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right)\\
=\nabla\cdot\left(\Sigma(v)-2A\nabla M\odot\nabla M+\tfrac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{\top}}{\det F}\right)+\mu_{0}\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}M\,.

Here the subscripts
X=L^{2}_{t,x}
or
L^{2}_{x}
in the variational form
\bigl(\delta_{x}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{Y}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigr)_{X}
for
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{A},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{F},\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}
indicate the spaces in which the variations are carried out.

Define
P(\rho,M,\nabla M,H_{ext})=p(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}
and
\mathcal{T}(v,F,M,\nabla M,H_{ext})=\Sigma(v)-2A\nabla M\odot\nabla M+\tfrac{W^{\prime}(F)F^{T}}{\det F}
. Then we immediately obtain the equation for the velocity field
v
:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\rho(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v)+\nabla P=\nabla\cdot\mathcal{T}+\mu_{0}\nabla^{\top}H_{ext}M\,.

In addition, the mass equation reads

(2.13)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho v)=0\,.

2.2.
EnVarA for LLG equation of magnetization

We begin with the balance relation of angular momentum (see, e.g.,
[
2
,
53
]
):

(2.14)

\displaystyle g+h=\beta M\,,

where
g
is the kinematic transport of the magnetization vector
M
,
h=\delta_{M}\mathcal{F}
denotes the magnetic field, and the term
\beta
on the right-hand side is the Lagrangian multiplier for the unit-length constraint
|M|=1
. Note that
h
differs from the effective field
H_{\text{eff}}
by a minus sign, i.e.,
H_{\text{eff}}=-h
.

In the absence of damping, the kinematics of the magnetization vector
M
can be written as

(2.15)

\displaystyle\dot{M}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}M=-\gamma M\times H_{\mathrm{eff}}=\gamma M\times h\,,

with
\gamma>0
. The reader is referred to
[
4
]
for further details. Consequently, the total kinematic transport of
M
can be expressed as

(2.16)

\displaystyle\mathring{M}=\dot{M}-\gamma M\times h=\partial_{t}M+v\cdot\nabla M-\gamma M\times h\,,

which suggests that we write
g=\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\mathring{M}
. Multiplying (
2.14
) by
M
and using
g\cdot M=0
(a consequence of the unit-length constraint
|M|=1
), we obtain
\beta=h\cdot M
. Substituting this back into (
2.14
) yields

(2.17)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathring{M}=(h\cdot M)M-h

Using the vector triple product identity
a\times(b\times c)=(a\cdot c)b-(a\cdot b)c
, we have

(h\cdot M)M-h=(M\cdot h)M-(M\cdot M)h=M\times(M\times h).

Thus, (
2.17
) reduces to

(2.18)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}M+v\cdot\nabla M-\gamma M\times h=\lambda M\times(M\times h).

We next consider the variation with respect to the magnetization variable
M
:

(2.19)

\displaystyle\delta_{M}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{K}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=0,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\delta_{M}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left[w(\rho)+A|\nabla M|^{2}+\frac{W(F)}{\det F}-\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left(2A\nabla M:\nabla\delta M-\mu_{0}H_{ext}\cdot\delta M\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\left\langle-2A\Delta M-\mu_{0}H_{ext},\,\delta M\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\,.

Thus
h=\delta_{M}\mathcal{F}=-(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})
. Substituting this into (
2.18
) we finally obtain

(2.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}M+v\cdot\nabla M=-\gamma M\times(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})-\lambda M\times M\times(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})\,.

Remark 2.1

(Variation on magnetization dissipation)
.

We may explain (
2.14
) from the perspective of EnVarA. Consider the magnetization dissipation functional

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(M)=\int_{\Omega}\tfrac{1}{\lambda}|M\times\mathring{M}|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\,.

The variational process (maximum dissipation principle) with respect to
\mathring{M}
of the total transport derivative yields

(2.23)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathring{M}}(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\tfrac{1}{\lambda}(M\times\mathring{M})\cdot[M\times(\delta\mathring{M})]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\tfrac{1}{\lambda}[(M\times\mathring{M})\times M]\cdot(\delta\mathring{M})\mathrm{d}x=\left\langle\tfrac{1}{\lambda}(M\times\mathring{M})\times M,\,\delta\mathring{M}\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tfrac{1}{\lambda}[(M\cdot M)\mathring{M}-(M\cdot\mathring{M})M],\,\delta\mathring{M}\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}=\left\langle\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\mathring{M},\,\delta\mathring{M}\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}\,.

Analogously to (
2.10
), the force balance relation

\left(\delta_{M}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{K}\mathrm{d}t\right)_{L^{2}_{t,x}}-\left(\delta_{M}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{F}\mathrm{d}t\right)_{L^{2}_{t,x}}=\left(\delta_{\mathring{M}}\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}\right)_{L^{2}_{x}}

implies that

(2.24)

\displaystyle\delta_{M}\mathcal{F}+\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\mathring{M}=h+g=0\,.

This is exactly the angular momentum balance equation (
2.14
) without the Lagrange multiplier on the right-hand side.

Incidentally, by taking the tensor product of (
2.17
) with
M
, one readily verifies the equivalence between the magnetization dissipation rate functionals in (
2.22
) and that in (
1.1
).

3.
Local well-posedness with large initial data

In this section we study the local well-posedness of system (
1.8
) with initial data (
1.9
) using energy methods. We first derive a priori estimates for system (
1.8
) in higher-order Sobolev spaces. Then, following the works
[
9
,
8
,
11
]
, we construct a nonlinear iterative approximation scheme to prove the local existence of solutions to (
1.8
).

3.1.
A priori estimates for local well-posedness

In this subsection we derive the a priori estimates needed for the proof of local well-posedness of system (
1.8
). Recalling the definitions of
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
in (
1.10
), we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)

\displaystyle:=\|\rho\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|F\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|v\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\rho}}+\|\nabla M\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mu\|\nabla v\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+(\mu+\xi)\|\nabla\cdot v\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+2\lambda A\|\Delta M\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\,.

Then we have the following a priori estimates.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
s\geq 3
be an integer and let the external magnetic field satisfy
H_{\text{ext}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
. Let
\rho_{0}(x)
be the initial density as given in Theorem
1.1
. Assume that
(\rho,v,F,M)
is a sufficiently smooth solution to system (
1.8
). Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that

(3.1)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)+\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)\lesssim(1+\|H_{ext}\|^{2}_{H^{s}})(1+\|\tfrac{1}{\rho_{0}}\|^{s+1}_{L^{\infty}}e^{c(s+1)t\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)})\big(1+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{s}{2}+\gamma+3}(t)\big).

Next we focus on the derivation of the above estimate.

3.1.1.
Estimates for
\rho
-evolution

In this subsection, we present the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any multi-index
m\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
with
|m|\leq s
and
s\geq 3
, we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\tfrac{1}{\rho}\|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{3}{2}}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\,,

where
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
are defined in (
1.10
).

Proof.

We apply the
m
-th order derivative operator to the first
\rho
-equation in (
1.8
) for all
|m|\leq s
, take the
L^{2}
inner product with
\partial^{m}\rho
, and integrate by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
. This yields

(3.3)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=-\langle\partial^{m}(v\cdot\nabla\rho),\partial^{m}\rho\rangle-\langle\partial^{m}(\rho\nabla\cdot v),\partial^{m}\rho\rangle=I_{1}+I_{2}\,.

The term
I_{1}
can be decomposed as

(3.4)

\displaystyle I_{1}=

\displaystyle\underset{I_{11}}{\underbrace{-\langle v\cdot\nabla\partial^{m}\rho,\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}\underset{I_{12}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}=1\end{subarray},m^{\prime}\leq m}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle(\partial^{m^{\prime}}v\cdot\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}})\rho,\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{I_{13}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray},m^{\prime}\leq m}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle(\partial^{m^{\prime}}v\cdot\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}})\rho,\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}-\underset{I_{14}}{\underbrace{\langle\partial^{m}v\cdot\nabla\rho,\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}\,.

Using Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding theory, we obtain

(3.5)

\displaystyle I_{11}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla\cdot v,|\partial^{m}\rho|^{2}\rangle\lesssim\|\nabla\cdot v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,,

and

(3.6)

\displaystyle I_{12}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m,\\
|m^{\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}\|\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}p\|_{L^{2}}\,,

and

(3.7)

\displaystyle I_{13}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m,\\
2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray}}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{4}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\,,

and

(3.8)

\displaystyle I_{14}\lesssim\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Collecting the above estimates, we bound
I_{1}
as

(3.9)

\displaystyle I_{1}=

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}+\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Observe that

(3.10)

\displaystyle I_{2}=

\displaystyle\underset{I_{21}}{\underbrace{-\langle\partial^{m}(\rho\nabla\cdot v),\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}\underset{I_{22}}{\underbrace{-\langle\rho\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}v,\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{I_{23}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}=1\end{subarray},m^{\prime}\leq m}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle(\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\nabla\cdot\partial^{m-m^{\prime}})v,\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{I_{24}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray},m^{\prime}\leq m}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle(\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\nabla\cdot\partial^{m-m^{\prime}})v,\partial^{m}\rho\rangle}}\,.

By Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding, we have

\displaystyle I_{21}\lesssim\|\nabla\cdot v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,,

and

\displaystyle I_{22}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\,,

and

\displaystyle I_{23}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
|m^{\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\cdot\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\quad\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,,

and

\displaystyle I_{24}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla\cdot\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{4}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\quad\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Together with all the above estimates, the quantity
I_{2}
can be bounded by

(3.11)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Combining (
3.3
), (
3.9
), and (
3.11
), we obtain

(3.12)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\tfrac{1}{\rho}\|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H_{\rho}^{s}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\,,

where we have used the fact that
\|v\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{1/2}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}
. Together with the definitions of
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
in (
1.10
), the estimate (
3.12
) completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

3.1.2.
Estimates for
F
-evolution

In this subsection, we present the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any multi-index
m\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
with
|m|\leq s
and
s\geq 3
, we have

(3.13)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}F\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\tfrac{1}{\rho}\|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{3}{2}}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\,,

where
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
are defined in (
1.10
).

Proof.

We apply the
m
-th order derivative operator
\partial^{m}
to the third equation for
F
in (
1.8
) for all
|m|\leq s
, take the
L^{2}
inner product with
\partial^{m}F
, and integrate by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
. This yields

(3.14)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}F\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=-\langle\partial^{m}(v\cdot\nabla F),\partial^{m}F\rangle+\langle\partial^{m}(\nabla vF),\partial^{m}F\rangle\,.

By similar arguments as in (
3.9
), we obtain

(3.15)

\displaystyle-\langle\partial^{m}(v\cdot\nabla F),\partial^{m}F\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}F\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}+\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\|F\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}\,.

An analogous calculation as in (
3.11
) gives

(3.16)

\displaystyle\langle\partial^{m}(\nabla vF),\partial^{m}F\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}F\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|F\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|F\|_{H^{3}}\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}+\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Combining (
3.14
),(
3.15
) and (
3.16
), we have

(3.17)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}F\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}F\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}+\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}+\|F\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}+\|F\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\tfrac{1}{\rho}\|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}F\|_{L^{2}}+\|F\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Together with the definitions of
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
, estimate (
3.17
) implies the bound (
3.13
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

3.1.3.
Estimates for
v
-evolution

In this subsection, we present the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any multi-index
m\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
with
|m|\leq s
and
s\geq 3
, we have

(3.18)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}^{2}+\mu\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(\mu+\xi)\|\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{E}_{s}^{2}(t)+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)

\displaystyle+\big[\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{3}{2}}(t)+(1+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t))\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}\big]\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\,,

where
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
are defined in (
1.10
).

Proof.

We apply the
m
-th order derivative operator
\partial^{m}
to the second equation for
v
in (
1.8
) for all
|m|\leq s
, take the
L^{2}
inner product with
\partial^{m}v
, and integrate by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
. This yields

(3.19)

\displaystyle\underset{V_{1}}{\underbrace{\langle\partial^{m}[\rho(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v)],\partial^{m}v\rangle}}+\underset{V_{2}}{\underbrace{\langle\nabla\partial^{m}\left(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right),\partial^{m}v\rangle}}

\displaystyle+\mu\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+(\mu+\xi)\|\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{V_{3}}{\underbrace{\langle\partial^{m}(\rho F:\nabla F^{\top}),\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\underset{V_{4}}{\underbrace{-2A\langle\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}(\nabla M\odot\nabla M),\partial^{m}v\rangle}}

\displaystyle+\underset{V_{5}}{\underbrace{\mu_{0}\langle\partial^{m}(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M,\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\,.

We then estimate the quantities
V_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq 5
) term by term.

Observe that
V_{1}
can be further decomposed as

(3.20)

\displaystyle V_{1}=

\displaystyle\langle\partial^{m}[\rho(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v)],\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\rho(\partial_{t}\partial^{m}v+\partial^{m}(v\cdot\nabla v)),\partial^{m}v\rangle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq m^{\prime}\leq m\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v),\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{p}}\underset{V_{11}}{\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{t}\rho,|\partial^{m}v|^{2}\rangle}}+\underset{V_{12}}{\underbrace{\langle\rho\partial^{m}(v\cdot\nabla),\partial^{m}v\rangle}}

\displaystyle+\underset{V_{13}}{\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq m^{\prime}\leq m\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\partial^{m}v,\partial^{m}v\rangle}}

\displaystyle+\underset{V_{14}}{\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq m^{\prime}\leq m\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(v\cdot\nabla v,,\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\,.

Together with the
\rho
-equation in (
1.8
), the term
V_{11}
can be bounded using Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding:

(3.21)

\displaystyle-V_{11}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{t}\rho,|\partial^{m}v|^{2}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\langle v\cdot\nabla\rho+\rho\nabla\cdot v,|\partial^{m}v|^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim(\||v\|_{L^{\infty}}\||\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}+\||\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\||\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}})\||\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H^{3}_{\rho}}\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\,.

The term
V_{12}
can be split into finer parts:

\displaystyle-V_{12}

\displaystyle=-\langle\rho\partial^{m}(v\cdot\nabla),\partial^{m}v\rangle

(3.22)

\displaystyle=\underset{V_{121}}{\underbrace{-\langle\rho v\cdot\nabla\partial^{m}v,\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\underset{V_{122}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m,|m^{\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle\rho\partial^{m^{\prime}}v\cdot\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v,\partial^{m}v\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{V_{123}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m,2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray}}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle\rho\partial^{m^{\prime}}v\cdot\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v,\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\underset{V_{124}}{\underbrace{-\langle\rho\partial^{m}v\cdot\nabla v,\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\,.

Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding then give

(3.23)

\displaystyle V_{121}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla\cdot(\rho v)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|v\|_{H_{\rho}^{3}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}^{2}\,,

and

(3.24)

\displaystyle V_{122}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
|m^{\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}\|\rho\partial^{m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|v\|_{H^{3}_{\rho}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}^{2}\,,

and

(3.25)

\displaystyle V_{123}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray}}\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{4}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\,,

and

(3.26)

\displaystyle V_{124}\lesssim\|\rho\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|v\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|v\|_{H^{3}_{\rho}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}^{2}\,.

Consequently,
V_{12}
satisfies the bound

(3.27)

\displaystyle-V_{12}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H^{3}_{\rho}}\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\rho}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}

\displaystyle+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H^{3}_{\rho}}\|\rho\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\,.

Similarly, using Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding, the term
V_{13}
can be estimated as

(3.28)

\displaystyle-V_{13}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq m^{\prime}\leq m\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C^{m^{\prime}}_{m}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\partial^{m}v,\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m,|m^{\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{t}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m,2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{4}}\|\partial_{t}\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}v\|_{L^{4}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\left(\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\right)\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}\,.

We now turn to
V_{14}
. It can be decomposed as follows:

\displaystyle-V_{14}=

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq m^{\prime}\leq m\\
|m|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(v\cdot\nabla v),\partial^{m}v\rangle

(3.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{V_{141}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
|m^{\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(v\cdot\nabla v),\partial^{m}v\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underset{V_{142}}{\underbrace{-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(v\cdot\nabla v),\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\ \underset{V_{143}}{\underbrace{-\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}\langle\partial^{m}\rho(v\cdot\nabla v),\partial^{m}v\rangle}}\,.

By Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding, we obtain

(3.30)

\displaystyle V_{141}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
|m^{\prime}|=1\end{subarray}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(v\cdot\nabla v)\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|v\cdot\nabla v\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L_{\rho}^{2}}\,,

and

(3.31)

\displaystyle V_{142}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
2\leq|m^{\prime}|\leq s-1\end{subarray}}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\|_{L^{4}}\|\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(v\cdot\nabla v)\|_{L^{4}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\cdot\nabla v\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L_{\rho}^{2}}\,,

and

(3.32)

\displaystyle V_{143}\lesssim\|\partial^{m}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\|v\cdot\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{3}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{3}_{\rho}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L_{\rho}^{2}}\,.

Thus
V_{14}
admits the bound

(3.33)

\displaystyle-V_{14}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq m^{\prime}\leq m\\
|m|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{|m|\geq 1}C_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\langle\partial^{m^{\prime}}\rho\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}(v\cdot\nabla v),\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{p}}^{2}\left(\|\rho\|_{H^{3}}\|v\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\rho}}+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}^{2}+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{3}_{\rho}}^{2}\right)\,.

Under the assumption
s\geq 3
, collecting the estimates (
3.21
), (
3.27
), (
3.28
) and (
3.33
) yields the following lower bound for
V_{1}
:

(3.34)

\displaystyle V_{1}=

\displaystyle\langle\partial^{m}\left[\rho\left(\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v\right)\right],\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}^{2}-C\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\rho}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L_{\rho}}^{2}

\displaystyle-C\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}

for some positive constant
C>0
.

Next we estimate the quantity
V_{2}
. It is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle-V_{2}

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla\partial^{m}(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}),\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\partial^{m}(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}),\nabla\partial^{m}v\rangle

(3.35)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial^{m}(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext})\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|\partial^{m}P(\rho)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\partial^{m}(|\nabla M|^{2})\|_{L^{2}}+\|\partial^{m}(M\cdot H_{ext})\|_{L^{2}}\right)\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Recalling
P(\rho)=a\rho^{\gamma}
with
\gamma\geq 1
, we have

\|\partial^{m}P(\rho)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}^{\gamma}

for
|m|\leq s
. Moreover, if
s\geq 3
,

\|\partial^{m}|\nabla M|^{2}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\,,

and

\displaystyle\|\partial^{m}(M\cdot H_{ext})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|M\cdot\partial^{m}H_{ext}\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{0\neq m^{\prime}\leq m}\|\partial^{m^{\prime}}M\cdot\partial^{m-m^{\prime}}H_{ext}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial^{m}H_{ext}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s-1}}\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s-1}}\,,

where we have used the fact that
|M|=1
. Consequently, we obtain

(3.36)

\displaystyle-V_{2}

\displaystyle-\langle\nabla\partial^{m}(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}),\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|P\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+(1+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}})\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}\right)\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}.

For the quantity
V_{3}
, integration by parts together with Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding yields, for
s\geq 3
,

(3.37)

\displaystyle V_{3}

\displaystyle=\langle\partial^{m}(\rho F:\nabla F^{\top}),\partial^{m}v\rangle=\langle\partial^{m}(\rho F^{jk}\partial_{j}F^{ik}),\partial^{m}v^{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}(\rho FF^{\top}),\partial^{m}v\rangle=\langle\partial_{j}\partial^{m}(\rho F^{ik}F^{jk}),\partial^{m}v^{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\partial^{m}(\rho F^{ik}F^{jk}),\partial^{m}\partial_{j}v^{i}\rangle\lesssim\|\partial^{m}(\rho FF^{\top})\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}.

Similarly, the quantity
V_{4}
can be bounded by

(3.38)

\displaystyle V_{4}

\displaystyle=-2A\langle\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}(\nabla M\odot\nabla M),\partial^{m}v\rangle=2A\langle\partial^{m}(\nabla M\odot\nabla M),\nabla\partial^{m}v\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial^{m}(\nabla M\odot\nabla M)\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Furthermore, the quantity
V_{5}
can be estimated analogously:

(3.39)

\displaystyle V_{5}

\displaystyle=\mu_{0}\langle\partial^{m}[(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M],\partial^{m}v\rangle=\mu_{0}\langle\partial^{m}(\partial_{i}H_{ext}^{j}M_{j}),\partial^{m}v^{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\mu_{0}\langle\partial^{m}(H_{ext}^{i}\partial_{i}M_{j}),\partial^{m}v^{i}\rangle-\mu_{0}\langle\partial^{m}(H_{ext}^{i}M_{j}),\partial^{m}\partial_{i}v^{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial^{m}(\nabla M\cdot H_{ext})\|_{L^{2}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}+\|\partial^{m}(M\cdot H_{ext})\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}+(1+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}})\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Substituting the estimates (
3.34
), (
3.36
), (
3.37
), (
3.38
) and (
3.39
) into (
3.19
), we obtain for
s\geq 3

(3.40)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}^{2}+\mu\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(\mu+\xi)\|\nabla\cdot\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}^{2}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}_{\rho}}

\displaystyle+[\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}^{\gamma}+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+(1+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}})\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}]\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\left[\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+(1+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}})\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}\right]\|\nabla\partial^{m}v\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Together with the definitions of
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
in (
1.10
), inequality (
3.40
) implies the desired estimate (
3.18
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

3.1.4.
Estimates for
\partial_{t}v

The goal of this subsection is to estimate the norm
\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}
appearing in (
3.18
). The idea is to bound the time derivative
\partial_{t}v
in terms of the spatial derivatives of
(\rho,v,F,M)
by exploiting the structure of the
v
-equation in (
1.8
). More precisely, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

For
\gamma>1
and integer
s\geq 3
, we have

(3.41)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim(1+\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}})(\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{s}{2}}_{L^{\infty}})\big(1+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{s+\gamma}{2}}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{s+2}{2}}(t)+\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\big)\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\,,

where
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}_{s}(t)
are defined in (
1.10
).

Proof.

The
v
-equation in (
1.8
) can be rewritten as

(3.42)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+\;v\cdot\nabla v+\frac{1}{\rho}{\nabla\left(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{\rho}\Delta v+\frac{\mu+\xi}{\rho}\nabla(\nabla\cdot v)+)\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\cdot(\rho FF^{\top})-\frac{2A}{\rho}\nabla\cdot(\nabla M\odot\nabla M)+\frac{\mu_{0}}{\rho}(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M\,.

We then obtain

(3.43)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\cdot\nabla v\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\left(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right)\|_{H^{s-1}}

\displaystyle+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\Delta v\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\cdot v\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\cdot(\rho FF^{\top})\|_{H^{s-1}}

\displaystyle+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\cdot(\nabla M\odot\nabla M)\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M\|_{H^{s-1}}\,.

By applying Lemma 3.2 of
[
11
]
or Lemma 3.1 of
[
13
]
, we obtain for
s\geq 3
,

(3.44)

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot f\|_{H^{s-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{s-1}{2}})\|\nabla\rho\|_{H^{s-2}}(1+\|\nabla\rho\|^{s-2}_{H^{s-2}})\|f\|_{H^{s-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\underset{K_{s}(\rho)}{\underbrace{(\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{s-1}{2}})\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}(1+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}^{s-2})}}\|f\|_{H^{s-1}}\,.

Using (
3.44
), we have

(3.45)

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\left(P(\rho)-A|\nabla M|^{2}+\mu_{0}M\cdot H_{ext}\right)\|_{H^{s-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\left(\|\nabla P(\rho)\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\nabla|\nabla M|^{2}\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|M\cdot H_{ext}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)(\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}^{\gamma}+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+(1+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}})\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}})\,.

Similarly,

(3.46)

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\Delta v\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\Delta v\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\nabla v\|_{H^{s}}\,,

and

(3.47)

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\cdot v\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\nabla\cdot v\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|v\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}^{2}\,,

and

(3.48)

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\cdot(\rho FF^{\top})\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\nabla\cdot(\rho FF^{\top})\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\,,

and

(3.49)

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\cdot(\nabla M\odot\nabla M)\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\nabla\cdot(\nabla M\odot\nabla M)\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\,,

and

(3.50)

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)\|(\nabla H_{ext})^{\top}M\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim K_{s}(\rho)(1+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}})\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}\,.

Substituting the estimates (
3.45
), (
3.46
), (
3.47
), (
3.48
), (
3.49
), and (
3.50
) into (
3.43
) yields, for
s\geq 3
,

(3.51)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}v\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\rho}}+K_{s}(\rho)\|\nabla v\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle+K_{s}(\rho)(\|\rho\|^{\gamma}_{H_{s}}+\|\nabla M\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+(1+\|\nabla M\|_{H^{s}})\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}})

\displaystyle+K_{s}(\rho)(\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\rho}}+\|\rho\|_{H^{s}}\|F\|^{2}_{H^{s}})\,.

Note that

\displaystyle K_{s}(\rho)\lesssim(\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{s-1}{2}})(1+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{s-1}{2}}(t))\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\,.

Inequality (
3.51
) implies the desired bound (
3.41
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

3.1.5.
Estimates for the
M
-equation

The goal of this subsection is to establish energy estimates for the magnetic field
M
. We have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any multi-index
m\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
with
|m|\leq s
and
s\geq 3
, we have

(3.52)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla\partial^{m}M\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+2A\lambda\|\Delta\partial^{m}M\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{\rho}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{L^{\infty}}\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)+\left[(1+\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t))\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)+(1+\mathcal{E}_{s}(t))\|H_{ext}\|_{H^{s}}\right]\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\,.

Proof.

We apply the
m
-th order derivative operator
\partial^{m}
to the
M
-equation in (
1.8
) for all
|m|\leq s
, take the
L^{2}
inner product with
\Delta\partial^{m}M
, and integrate by parts over
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
. This yields

(3.53)

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}\partial^{m}M,\Delta\partial^{m}M\rangle+\langle\partial^{m}(v\cdot\nabla M),\Delta\partial^{m}M\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2A\lambda\|\Delta\partial^{m}M\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lambda\mu_{0}\langle\partial^{m}H_{ext},\Delta\partial^{m}M\rangle+\langle\partial^{m}(\Gamma(M)M),\Delta\partial^{m}M\rangle

\displaystyle-2\gamma\langle\partial^{m}\left[M\times(2A\Delta M+\mu_{0}H_{ext})\right],\Delta\partial^{m}M\rangle\,.

Observe that

(3.54)

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}\partial^{m}M,\Delta\partial^{m}M\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\nabla\partial^{m}M\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,.

Moreover, Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding give

(3.55)
